, if party
\mathcal{P}\in\{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S}\}
has misbehaved in the
j
-th verification (i.e., provided invalid query or service proof). It also updates
\bm{y}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{P}}
(by setting an element of it to one) if party
\mathcal{P}
has provided a complain that does not allow it to identify a misbehaved party, in the
j
-th verification, i.e., when the arbiter is unnecessarily invoked.

\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{pay}(\bm{y},T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},a,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})\rightarrow({\bm{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},{\bm{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},

\bm{coin}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}})
: It is run by the smart contract. It takes as input the binary vectors
[\bm{y}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\bm{y}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},

\bm{y}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}]\in\bm{y}
that indicate which party misbehaved, or sent invalid complaint in each verification,
T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}:=\{cp,g_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}\}
,
a
, the total coins the server should deposit
p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
,
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
, and
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
. If
a=1
and
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
, then it verifies the validity of
T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}
. If
T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}
is rejected, then it aborts.
If it is accepted, then it constructs vector
{\bm{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{P}}
, where
\mathcal{P}\in\{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{R}\}
; It sends
{{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{P},j}\in{\bm{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{P}}
coins to party
\mathcal{P}
for each
j
-th verification. Otherwise (i.e.,
a=0
or
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\neq p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
) it sends
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
coins to
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{S}
respectively.

The above algorithms
\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{genQuery},\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{prove},

\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{verify}
, and
\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{resolve}
implicitly take
(a,coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})
as other inputs and execute only if
a=1
and
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
; but, for simplicity we avoided explicitly stating it in the definition.
An RC-S-P scheme must meet correctness and security. Correctness requires that by the end of the protocol’s execution (that involves honest client and server), the server accepts an honest client’s encoded data and query while the honest client accepts the server’s valid service proof (and no one is identified as a misbehaving party). Moreover, the honest client gets back all its deposited coins minus the service payment, the honest server gets back all its deposited coins plus the service payment and the arbiter (that is not involved) receives nothing. Correctness is formally stated below.

Definition 2

(Correctness)
.

An RC-S-P scheme with functions
F,M,E,D,Q
is
correct
for auxiliary information

aux
if for any
z
polynomial in
\lambda
, any price list
pl
, and any service input
u
, it holds that the following probability is equal to
1
:

\Pr\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{keyGen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\bm{k}\\
\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{cInit}(1^{\lambda},u,\bm{k},z,pl)\rightarrow(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},e,T,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}})\\
\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{sInit}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},e,pk,z,T,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y})\rightarrow(coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},a)\\
\mathsf{For}\;j=1,\ldots,z\;\mathsf{do}:\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{genQuery}(1^{\lambda},\text{aux},k,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{prove}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\sigma,c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},pk,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow(b_{\scriptscriptstyle j},m_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{verify}(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},k,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow(d_{\scriptscriptstyle j},\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j})\\
\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{resolve}(\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},z,{\bm{\pi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},{\bm{c}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},pk,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow\bm{y}\\
\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{pay}(\bm{y},T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},a,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})\rightarrow(\bm{coin}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\\
\bm{coin}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{coin}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}})\\
\hline\cr(a=1)\wedge(\bigwedge\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle z}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\bigwedge\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle z}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}d_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=1)\ \wedge\\
(\bm{y}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}=\bm{y}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=\bm{y}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}=\bm{y}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=0)\ \wedge\\
(\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle z}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}=coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}-o\cdot z)\ \wedge\\
(\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle z}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}=coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}+o\cdot z)\ \wedge(\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle z}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R},j}=0)\\
\end{array}\right]

where
\bm{y}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\bm{y}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\bm{y}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\in\bm{y}
.

An RC-S-P scheme is said to be secure if it satisfies three main properties: (i) security against a malicious server, (ii) security against a malicious client, and (cii privacy. In the following, we formally define each of them.

Intuitively, security against a malicious server states that, for each
j
-th verification, the adversary wins only with a negligible probability, if it provides either (a) correct evaluation of the function on the service input but it either makes the client withdraw an incorrect amount of coins (i.e., something other than its deposit minus service payment) or makes the arbiter withdraw an incorrect amount of coins if it unnecessarily invokes the arbiter, or (b) incorrect evaluation of the function on the service input, but either persuades the client or the arbiter to accept it or makes them withdraw an incorrect amount of coins (i.e.,
coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}\neq\frac{coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}}{z}
or
coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R},j}\neq l
coins). Below, we formalize this intuition.

Definition 3

(Security Against Malicious Server)
.

An RC-S-P scheme with functions
F,M,E,D,Q
is
secure against a malicious server
for auxiliary information
aux
, if for any
z
polynomial in
\lambda
, any price list
pl
, every
j
(where
1\leq j\leq z
), and any PPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
, it holds that the following probability is
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
:

\Pr\left[{\small{\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP.keyGen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\bm{k}\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda},pk,F,M,E,D,Q,z,pl)\rightarrow u\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{cInit}(1^{\lambda},u,\bm{k},z,pl)\rightarrow(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},e,T,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}})\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{A}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},e,pk,z,T,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y})\rightarrow(coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},a)\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{genQuery}(1^{\lambda},\text{aux},k,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{A}(c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},\sigma,u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},a)\rightarrow(b_{\scriptscriptstyle j},m_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j},h^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},\delta^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{verify}(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},k,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow(d_{\scriptscriptstyle j},\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j})\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{resolve}(\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},z,{\bm{\pi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},{\bm{c}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},pk,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow\bm{y}\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{pay}(\bm{y},T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},a,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})\rightarrow({\bm{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\\
{\bm{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},{\bm{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}})\\
\hline\cr\Big(F(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},{pp})=h_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\ \wedge(coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}\neq\frac{coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}}{z}-o\vee\\
(coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R},j}\neq l\ \wedge\ y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}=1))\Big)\ \vee\\
\Big(F(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},{pp})\neq h_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\ \wedge(d_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=1\vee y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}=0\vee\\
\ coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}\neq\frac{coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}}{z}\ \vee coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R},j}\neq l)\Big)\\
\end{array}}}\right]

where
\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\in D(c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})
,
\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=[h^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},\delta^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}],h_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=D(h^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})
,
\sigma\in e
,
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}\in\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},m_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}\in\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}\in\bm{y}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\in\bm{y}
,
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}\in\bm{y}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\in\bm{y}
, and
{pp}\in T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
.

Informally, security against a malicious client requires that, for each
j
-th verification, a malicious client with a negligible probability wins if it provides either (a) valid metadata and query but either makes the server receive an incorrect amount of coins (something other than its deposit plus the service payment), or makes the arbiter withdraw incorrect amounts of coin if it unnecessarily invokes the arbiter or (b) invalid metadata or query but convinces the server to accept either of them (i.e., the invalid metadata or query), or (c) invalid query but persuades the arbiter to accept it, or makes them withdraw an incorrect amount of coins (i.e.,
coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}\neq\frac{coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}}{z}+o
or
coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R},j}\neq l
coins). Below, we formally state the property. Note that in the following definition, an honest server either does not deposit (e.g., when
a=0
) or if it deposits (i.e., agrees to serve) ultimately receives its deposit
plus the service payment
(with high probability).

Definition 4

(Security Against Malicious Client)
.

An RC-S-P scheme with functions
F,M,E,D,Q
is
secure against a malicious client
for auxiliary information
aux
, if for any
z
polynomial in
\lambda
, any price list
pl
, every
j
(where
1\leq j\leq z
), and any PPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
, it holds that the following probability is
\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
:

\Pr\left[{\small{\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda},F,M,E,D,Q,z,pl)\rightarrow(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\bm{k},e,T,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\\
\bm{y},pk)\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{sInit}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},e,pk,z,T,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y})\rightarrow(coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},a)\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{A}(coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},a,1^{\lambda},\text{aux},k,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{prove}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\sigma,c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},pk,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow(b_{\scriptscriptstyle j},m_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{A}(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},k,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow(d_{\scriptscriptstyle j},\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{C}},j})\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{resolve}(\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},z,{\bm{\pi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},{\bm{c}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},pk,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow\bm{y}\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{pay}(\bm{y},T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},a,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})\rightarrow({\bm{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\\
{\bm{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},{\bm{coin}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}})\\
\hline\cr\hskip-5.69054pt\Big((M(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},k,{pp})=\sigma\ \wedge Q(\text{aux},k,{pp})=\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\ \wedge\\
\hskip-5.69054pt(coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}\neq\frac{coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}}{z}+o\ \vee coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R},j}\neq l\ \wedge\ y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}=1)\Big)\vee\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\Big(M(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},k,{pp})\neq\sigma\wedge a=1\Big)\ \vee\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\Big(Q(\text{aux},k,{pp})\neq\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\ \wedge(b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=1\ \vee\\
\ y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}=0\ \vee coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}\neq\frac{coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}}{z}+o\ \vee coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R},j}\neq l)\Big)\\
\end{array}}}\right]

where
\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\in D(c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},t_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})
,
\sigma\in e
,
y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}\in\bm{y}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}\in\bm{y}
,
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}\in\bm{y}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}\in\bm{y}
, and
{pp}\in T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
.

Informally, RC-S-P is privacy-preserving if it guarantees the privacy of (1) the service input (e.g., outsourced file) and (2) the service proof’s status during the private time bubble. In the following, we formally define privacy.

Definition 5

(Privacy)
.

An RC-S-P scheme with functions
F,M,E,

D,Q
preserves
privacy
for auxiliary information

aux
if for any
z
polynomial in
\lambda
and any price list
pl
, the following hold:

1.

For any PPT adversary
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
, it holds that the following probability is no more than
\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

\Pr\left[{\small{\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP.keyGen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\bm{k}\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(1^{\lambda},pk,F,M,E,D,Q,z,pl)\rightarrow(u_{\scriptscriptstyle 0},u_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\beta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\{0,1\}\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{cInit}(1^{\lambda},u_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle\beta}},\bm{k},z,pl)\rightarrow(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle\beta}},e,T,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y},\\
\hskip-5.69054ptcoin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}})\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{sInit}(u_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle\beta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},e,pk,z,T,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y})\rightarrow(coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},a)\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathsf{For}\;j=1,\ldots,z\;\mathsf{do}:\\
\hskip-9.95845pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{genQuery}(1^{\lambda},\text{aux},k,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\\
\hskip-9.95845pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{prove}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\beta},\sigma,c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},pk,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow(b_{\scriptscriptstyle j},m_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\\
\hskip-9.95845pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{verify}(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},k,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow(d_{\scriptscriptstyle j},\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j})\\
\hline\cr\hskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\bm{c}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},g_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},g_{\scriptscriptstyle qp},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},pl,a)\rightarrow\beta\\
\end{array}}}\right]

where
\bm{c}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}=[{c}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,{c}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}]
and
\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}=[\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}]
.

2.

For any PPT adversaries
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
,
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
the following probability is no more than
Pr_{\mathrm{max}}+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
:

\Pr\left[{\small{\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP.keyGen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow\bm{k}\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(1^{\lambda},pk,F,M,E,D,Q,z,pl)\rightarrow u\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{cInit}(1^{\lambda},u,\bm{k},M,z,pl,enc)\rightarrow(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},e,T,\\
\hskip-5.69054ptp_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}})\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{sInit}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},e,pk,z,T,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y})\rightarrow(coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},a)\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\mathsf{For}\;j=1,\ldots,z\;\mathsf{do}:\\
\hskip-9.95845pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(1^{\lambda},\text{aux},k,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\\
\hskip-9.95845pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\sigma,c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},pk,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow(b_{\scriptscriptstyle j},m_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\\
\hskip-9.95845pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{verify}(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},k,T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})\rightarrow(d_{\scriptscriptstyle j},\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j})\\
\hline\cr\hskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}(F,M,E,D,Q,\bm{c}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},g_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},g_{\scriptscriptstyle qp},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\\
pl,a)\rightarrow(d_{\scriptscriptstyle j},j)\\
\end{array}}}\right]

where
\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
has been encoded correctly,
\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=[h^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},\delta^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}],h_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=D(h^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})
, and
Pr_{\mathrm{max}}
is defined as follows. Let
Exp_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{priv}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{3}}(1^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda})
be the above experiment. Let
\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\in D(c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})
,
{pp}\in T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
. We define the events
Con_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}:Q(\text{aux},k,pp)\neq\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
,
Con_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}:b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=0,Con_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}:Q(\text{aux},k,pp)=\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
,
Con_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}:b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=1
,
\overline{Con}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}:F(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},{pp})\neq h_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
,
\overline{Con}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}:d_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=0
,
\overline{Con}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}:F(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\bm{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},{pp})=h_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, and
\overline{Con}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}:d_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=1
. For
i\in\{0,1\}
and
j\in[z]
, let

Pr_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}:=\Pr\left[{\small{\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip 8.53581ptExp_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{priv}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{3}}(1^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda})\\
\hline\cr\Big(Con_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\ \wedge\ Con_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}\Big)\vee\\
\Big(\overline{Con}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}\ \wedge\ \overline{Con}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}\Big)\end{array}}}\right].\mbox{ Then,}

Pr_{\mathrm{max}}:=\mathrm{max}\{Pr_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,1},Pr_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,1},...,Pr_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,z},Pr_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,z}\}
.

In the above definition, for each
j
-th verification, the adversary
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
or
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
produces an invalid query or invalid proof, respectively, with probability
Pr_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,j}
and a valid query or valid proof, respectively, with probability
Pr_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,j}
. It is required that privacy is preserved regardless of the queries and proofs status, i.e., whether they are valid/invalid, as long as they are correctly encoded and provided. In the above definitions, the private time bubble is a time period from the point when
\mathtt{RCSP.keyGen}(\cdot)
is executed up to the time when
\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{resolve}(\cdot)
is run. In other words, the privacy holds up to the point where
\mathtt{RCSP}.\mathtt{resolve}(.)
is run. This is why the latter algorithm is excluded from the experiments in Definition
5
.

Definition 6

(RC-S-P Security)
.

An RC-S-P with functions
F,M,

E,D,Q
is
secure
for auxiliary information
aux
, if it satisfies security against malicious server, security against malicious client, and preserves privacy for
aux
, w.r.t. Definitions
3
,
4
, and
5
, respectively.

VIII
Generic RC-S-P Protocol

In this section, we outline the designs of the RC-S-P protocol that realises Definition
6
. As we stated in Section
VI
, this protocol relies on the idea that the server and client can efficiently agree on private statements at the beginning of the protocol. We first present a primitive, called
statement agreement protocol
(SAP), that satisfies the above requirement, and then present the RC-S-P.

VIII-A

Statement Agreement Protocol (SAP)

An SAP is secure if it meets four security properties:

1.

Neither party can persuade a third party verifier that it agreed with its counter-party on an invalid statement, i.e., a statement that not both parties have agreed on.

2.

After they agree on the statement, an honest party can always prove to the verifier that it has the agreement.

3.

The privacy of the statement should be preserved (from the public) before either of the two parties attempts to prove the agreement on the statement.

4.

After both parties reach an agreement, neither can later deny the agreement.

To that end, we use a combination of a smart contract (including digital signatures involved) and a commitment scheme. The idea is as follows. Let
x
be the statement. The client picks a random value and uses it to commit to
x
. It sends the commitment to the contract and the commitment opening (i.e., statement and the random value) to the server. The server checks if the opening matches the commitment and if so, it commits to the statement using the same random value and sends its commitment to the contract. Later, for a party to prove to the contract/verifier that it has agreed on the statement with the other party, it only sends the opening of the commitment. The contract/verifier checks if the opening matches both commitments and accepts if it matches. The SAP protocol is provided below. It assumes that each party
\mathcal{P}\in\{\mathcal{C,S}\}
already has a blockchain public address
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{P}}
.

1.

Initiate
.
\mathtt{SAP.init}(1^{\lambda},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},x)

The following steps are taken by
\mathcal{C}
.

(a)

Deploys a smart contract, SAP, that states both
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
. Let
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SAP}}
be the SC’s address.

(b)

Picks a random value
r
, and commits to the statement as
\mathtt{Com}(x,r)=g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
. It sends
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SAP}}
and
\ddot{x}:=(x,r)
to
\mathcal{S}
and sends
g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
to the contract.

2.

Agreement
.
\mathtt{SAP.agree}(x,r,g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SAP}})

The following steps are taken by
\mathcal{S}
.

(a)

Checks if
g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
was from
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathtt{Ver}(g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\ddot{x})=1
.

(b)

If the checks pass, it sets
b=1
, computes
\mathtt{Com}(x,r)=g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
, and sends
g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
to the contract. Else, it sets
b=0
and
g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=\bot
.

3.

Prove
. For
\mathcal{C}
(resp.
\mathcal{S}
) to prove that it has an agreement on
x
with
\mathcal{S}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}
), it sends
\ddot{x}:=(x,r)
to the contract.

4.

Verify
.
\mathtt{SAP.verify}(\ddot{x},g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})

The following steps are taken by the contract.

(a)

Ensures
g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
were sent from
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
.

(b)

Ensures
\mathtt{Ver}(g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\ddot{x})=\mathtt{Ver}(g_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\ddot{x})=1
.

(c)

Outputs
d=1
, if the checks in steps
4a
and
4b
pass. Otherwise, it outputs
d=0
.

In Appendix
I
, we discuss the SAP’s security and explain why naive solutions are not suitable.

VIII-B

Overview of RC-S-P Protocol

We have built the RC-S-P protocol using a novel combination of VSID, SAP, the private time bubble notion, symmetric-key encryption schemes, the coin masking and padding techniques. At a high level, it works as follows.

The client and server use SAP to provably agree on two private statements; the first statement includes payment details, while another one specifies a secret key, k, and the pads’ length. They also agree on public parameters such as (a) the private time bubble’s length, and (b) a smart contract specifying the parameters and the total amount of masked coins each party should deposit. The client deploys the contract. Each party deposits its masked coins in it. To start using the service, they invoke those VSID algorithms which let the server check if the client has generated its metadata correctly (via NIZK) and the server aborts if it decides not to serve.

At the end of each billing cycle, the client generates an encrypted query using
k
. It pads the encrypted query and sends the result to the contract.
In the same cycle, the server retrieves the query and checks its validity (via NIZK). If the query is rejected, the server locally stores the index of the billing cycle and then generates a dummy proof. Otherwise, if the server accepts the query, it generates a proof of service. In either case, the server encrypts the proof, pads it, and sends the result to the contract. After the server sends the messages to the contract, the client extracts and locally verifies the proof. If the verification passes, the client knows the server has delivered the service honestly. If the proof is rejected, it waits until the private time bubble passes and dispute resolution time arrives.

During the dispute resolution period, if the client or server rejects a proof, it invokes the arbiter, refers it to the invalid encrypted proofs in the contract, and sends to it the decryption key and the pads’ detail. The arbiter checks the key and pads validity. If they are accepted, then the arbiter extracts the related proofs and checks the validity of the party’s claim. The arbiter sends to the contract a report of its findings that includes the total number of times the server and client provided invalid proofs. To distribute the coins, the client or server sends: (a) “pay” message, (b) the agreed statement that specifies the payment details, and (c) the statement’s proof to the contract which verifies the statement and if approved it distributes the coins according to the statement’s
detail, and the arbiter’s report. Appendix
G
presents the RC-S-P protocol in detail.

IX
Recurring Contingent PoR Payment

In this section, we present recurring contingent PoR payment (RC-PoR-P) that is a concrete instantiation of the RC-S-P, when the verifiable service is PoR. We use PoR in the concrete instantiation of RC-S-P because PoR it is an active area of research and it will let us compare our solution to the-state-of-the-art, i.e., concrete instantiation of zkCSP of Campanelli
et al
.
[
15
]
.

In RC-PoR-P, instead of the function
F
, we have
F_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}}
which is an algorithm that takes as input
\mathcal{C}
’s encoded file
u^{*}
and
\mathcal{C}
’s query
\mathbf{q}
and outputs a proof asserting the outsourced data
u
is retrievable. For instance, if a PoR utilises a Merkle tree, then
F_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}}
is the algorithm that generates the Merkle tree’s proofs.
As a concrete instantiation, RC-PoR-P offers two primary added features. Specifically, unlike the generic RC-S-P construction (cf. Appendix
G
), it (a) does not use any zk proofs (even though either
\mathcal{C}
or
\mathcal{S}
can be malicious) which significantly improves costs, and (b) has a much lower arbiter-side computation cost; as we will show later, this also allows for a smart contract to efficiently play the arbiter’s role. Below, we first explain how the features are satisfied.

Avoiding the use of zk proofs
.
The majority of PoRs assume that only
\mathcal{S}
is potentially malicious while
\mathcal{C}
is honest. To ensure a file’s availability, they rely on metadata that is either a set of tags (e.g., MACs or signatures) or a root of a Merkle tree, built on the file blocks. In the case where
\mathcal{C}
can also be malicious, if tags are used then using zk proofs seem an obvious choice, as it allows
\mathcal{C}
to guarantee to
\mathcal{S}
that the tags have been constructed correctly (similar to the PoR in
[
6
]
). But, this imposes significant computation and communication costs. We observed that using a Merkle tree would benefit our protocol from a couple of perspectives; in short, it removes the need for zk proofs and it supports proof of misbehaviour. Our first observation is that if a Merkle tree is used, then
\mathcal{S}
can efficiently check the metadata’s correctness by reconstructing this tree on the file blocks, without involving zk proofs.

Low arbiter-side cost
.
In a Merkle tree-based PoR, in each verification, the number of proofs (or paths) are linear with the number of blocks that are probed, say
\phi
. In this scheme, the verifier checks all given proofs and rejects them if only one of them is invalid. We observed that if this scheme is used in the RC-PoR-P, then once
\mathcal{C}
finds an invalid proof, it can send only that single invalid proof as a
proof of misbehaviour
to the arbiter
\mathcal{R}
3
3

3

This idea is akin to the proof of misbehaviour proposed in
[
16
]
.
. This technique significantly reduces
\mathcal{R}
’s computation cost from
\phi\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(n)
to
\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(n)
, where
n
is the number of file blocks.

The RC-PoR-P scheme (cf. Subsection
IX-B
) deploys the following two building blocks:

1.

A PoR scheme, presented in Subsection
IX-A
, that can be seen as a variant of the standard Merkle tree-based PoR
[
34
,
50
,
38
]
. The security of the construction relies on the security of the underlying Merkle tree and pseudorandom function (cf. Subsection
III-B
).

2.

A
statement agreement protocol
(SAP), introduced in Subsection
VIII-A
, that lets
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{C}
efficiently agree on private statements at the beginning of the RC-PoR-P scheme. The SAP is built upon a binding and hiding commitment scheme, a smart contract, and a secure digital signature scheme used to sign transactions on the blockchain (cf. Subsections
III-B
and
III-A
).

IX-A

Modified Merkle tree-based PoR

In this section, we first present a modified version of the standard Merkle tree-based PoR and then explain the applied modifications. At a high level,
\mathcal{C}
encodes its input file using an error-correcting code, splits the result into blocks, and builds a Merkle tree on the blocks. Then, it locally stores the tree’s root and sends the blocks to
\mathcal{S}
which rebuilds the tree. At the verification time,
\mathcal{C}
sends a
\mathtt{PRF}
’s key to
\mathcal{S}
which derives a number of blocks’ indices showing which blocks are probed. For each probed block,
\mathcal{S}
generates a proof. It sends all proofs to
\mathcal{C}
which checks them. If
\mathcal{C}
accepts all proofs, then it concludes that its file is retrievable. Otherwise, if it rejects some proofs, it stores only one index of the blocks whose proofs were rejected. Below, we present the PoR protocol.

1.

Client-side Setup
.
\mathtt{PoR.setup}(1^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda},u)

(a)

\mathcal{C}
uses an error-correcting code, to encode the input file,
u
. Let
u^{\prime}
be the encoded file. Then, it splits
u^{\prime}
into
m
blocks as follows,
u^{\scriptscriptstyle*}=u^{{}^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}||1,...,u^{{}^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle m}||m
.

(b)

\mathcal{C}
constructs a Merkle tree on
u^{\scriptscriptstyle*}
’s blocks, i.e.,
\mathtt{MT.genTree}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*})
. Let
\sigma
be the root of the tree, and
\phi
be the number of blocks that will be probed. It sets public parameters as
pp:=(\sigma,\phi,m,\zeta)
, where
\zeta
is a
\mathtt{PRF}
’s description, as defined in Subsection
III-B
. It sends
pp
and
u^{\scriptscriptstyle*}
to
\mathcal{S}
.

2.

Client-side Query Generation
.
\mathtt{PoR.genQuery}(1^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda},pp)

(a)

\mathcal{C}
picks a key
\hat{k}
for
\mathtt{PRF}
and sends
\hat{k}
to
\mathcal{S}
.

3.

Server-side Proof Generation
.
\mathtt{PoR.prove}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\hat{k},pp)

(a)

\mathcal{S}
derives
\phi
pseudorandom indices as follows.

\forall i,1\leq i\leq\phi:q_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=\big(\mathtt{PRF}(\hat{k},i)\bmod m\big)+1
. Note that
1\leq q_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\leq m
. Let
{\bm{q}}=[q_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,q_{\scriptscriptstyle\phi}]
.

(b)

\mathcal{S}
generates a proof
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle{q_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}}}=\mathtt{MT.prove}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},q_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
, for each random index
q_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
. Let the final result be
{\bm{\pi}}=[(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle q_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}},

\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle q_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}})]_{\scriptscriptstyle q_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in{\bm{q}}}
, where
i
-th element in
{\bm{\pi}}
corresponds to
q_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, and the probed block is
u^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle q_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}}
. It sends
{\bm{\pi}}
to
\mathcal{C}
.

4.

Client-side Proof Verification
.
\mathtt{PoR.verify}({\bm{\pi}},\bm{q},pp)

(a)

If
|\bm{\pi}|=|\bm{q}|=1
, then
\mathcal{C}
sets
\phi=1
. This step is only for the case where a single proof and query is provided (e.g., in the proof of misbehaviour).

(b)

\mathcal{C}
checks if
\mathcal{S}
sent all proofs, by parsing each element of
\bm{\pi}
as:
\mathtt{parse}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle q_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}})=u^{{}^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle q_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}}||q_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}
, and checking if its index
q_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}
equals to
{\bm{q}}
’s
i
-th element (note,
\mathcal{C}
recomputes
{\bm{q}}
given
\hat{k}
). If all checks pass, it takes the next step. Else, it outputs
{\bm{d}}=[0,i]
, where
i
is the index of
{\bm{\pi}}
’s element that did not pass the check.

(c)

\mathcal{C}
checks if every path in
{\bm{\pi}}
is valid, by calling
\mathtt{MT.verify}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle q_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}},

\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle q_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}},\sigma)
. If all checks pass, it outputs
{\bm{d}}=[1,\bot]
; otherwise, it outputs
{\bm{d}}=[0,i]
, where
i
refers to the index of the first element in
{\bm{\pi}}
that does not pass the check.

The above protocol differs from the standard Merkle tree-based PoR from two perspectives; First, in step
4
,
\mathcal{C}
also outputs one of the rejected proofs’ indices. Given that index (and vectors of proofs and challenges), this will let a third party
efficiently
verify that
\mathcal{S}
did not pass the verification. Second, in step
2
, instead of sending
\phi
challenges, we let
\mathcal{C}
send only a key/seed of the
\mathtt{PRF}
to
\mathcal{S}
which can derive a set of challenges from it, such a technique has been used before, e.g., in
[
31
,
18
,
20
]
. This will lead to a decrease in the
\mathcal{C}
’s communication and smart contract’s storage costs.

IX-B

RC-PoR-P Protocol

In this section, we present our RC-PoR-P construction. The RC-PoR-P and the generic RC-S-P design share some ideas, yet as already mentioned, the two constructions have several differences. We provide the overview of the RC-PoR-P scheme and its detailed description below.

In the beginning,
\mathcal{C}
generates a symmetric encryption key
\bar{k}
and sets the number of dummy values to pad encrypted proofs,
pad_{\scriptscriptstyle\pi}
.
In its setup step,
\mathcal{C}
runs
\mathtt{PoR.setup}(1^{\lambda},u)
to obtain the encoding
u^{*}
and the parameters
pp:=(\sigma,\phi,m,\zeta)
. The query/proof secret parameters
qp
include
(\bar{k},pad_{\scriptscriptstyle\pi},pp)
.
\mathcal{C}
sets the coin secret parameters
cp:=(o,o_{\scriptscriptstyle max},l,l_{\scriptscriptstyle max},z)
(cf. Section
III
) that determine
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
, i.e. the total number of masked coins
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{S}
must deposit. It initiates two SAP sessions for agreements on
qp
and
cp
with
\mathcal{S}
and deploys a smart contract, SC. It completes setup by providing
\mathcal{S}
with
u^{*}
, the SAP parameters (including
qp
and
cp
), and the number of verifications,
z
, and depositing
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
coins in SC.
In server setup,
\mathcal{S}
checks whether a sufficient amount of coins has been deposited by
\mathcal{C}
and runs the agreement step of the two SAP sessions initiated by
\mathcal{C}
. If agreement is successful and the public parameters
(\sigma,\phi,m)
verify, it sends
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
coins to SC.

After their setup is complete,
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{S}
engage in the billing cycles phase for a number of
z
verifications as follows. During the
j
-th verification,
\mathcal{C}
runs
\mathtt{PoR.genQuery}
and sends the output query,
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, encrypted to SC. In turn,
\mathcal{S}
reads SC and decrypts the encrypted query. If
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
is invalid, it creates a complaint
m_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}
. Else, it runs
\mathtt{PoR.prove}
to generate a proof
{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
for
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
. Next, it sends
{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
encrypted and padded to SC. In order to verify,
\mathcal{C}
removes the pads and decrypts as
{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
and runs
\mathtt{PoR.verify}
for
{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
and
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
. If
{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
does not pass verification, it creates a complaint
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}
.

Dispute resolution takes place when
\mathcal{C}
rejects service proofs or
\mathcal{S}
rejects the queries. The arbiter
\mathcal{R}
receives the complaint vectors
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
from
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{S}
along with each party’s “views” of the two SAP sessions. Given
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
and the view of
\mathcal{S}
, if
\mathcal{S}
’s view is valid, then
\mathcal{R}
decides for every complaint in
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
by decrypting the corresponding query and executing
\mathcal{S}
’s steps for that query in the billing cycles phase described above. Given
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and the view of
\mathcal{C}
, if
\mathcal{C}
’s view is valid, then
\mathcal{R}
decides for every complaint in
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
by retrieving the rejected proof’s details (included in the complaint), decrypting the related query and (i) executing
\mathcal{S}
’s steps for that query, (ii) executing
\mathcal{C}
’s verification for the rejected proof and the related query. The arbiter updates SC’s state based upon its decisions. Finally, coin transfer is carried out according to the state of SC, as updated by
\mathcal{R}
.

Before we present the protocol, we discuss how metadata generator function
M_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}}
, the pair of encoding/decoding functions
(E_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}},

D_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}})
and the query generator function
Q_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}}
(involved in the RC-S-P Definition
1
) are defined in the PoR context, as they are often implicit in the original definition of PoR. Briefly,
M_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}}
is a function that processes a file and generates metadata. For instance, when PoR uses a Merkle tree, then
M_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}}
refers to

\mathtt{MT.genTree}(w)\rightarrow(tr,\sigma)
, where
tr
is the tree constructed on in file
w
and
\sigma
is the root of the tree. Encoding by
E_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}}
refers to encrypting with a symmetric key and then adding an appropriate number of pads, while decoding by
D_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}}
refers to removing the pads and then decrypting with the symmetric key.
Furthermore,
Q_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}}
can be a
\mathtt{PRF}
that generates a set of pseudorandom strings in a certain range, e.g., file block’s indices.

1.

Key Generation
.
\mathtt{RCPoRP}.\mathtt{keyGen}(1^{\lambda})

(a)

\mathcal{C}
picks a fresh symmetric encryption key
\bar{k}\leftarrow\mathtt{SKE.keyGen}(1^{\lambda})
.

(b)

\mathcal{C}
sets parameter
pad_{\scriptscriptstyle\pi}
: the number of dummy values to pad encrypted proofs. Let
sk^{\prime}:=(pad_{\scriptscriptstyle\pi},\bar{k})
. The key’s size is part of the security parameter. Let
{k^{\prime}}:=(sk^{\prime},pk^{\prime})
, where
pk^{\prime}:=(adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})
. The values of
pad_{\scriptscriptstyle\pi}
is determined as
pad_{\scriptscriptstyle\pi}=\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle max}-\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle act}
, where
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle max}
and
\pi_{\scriptscriptstyle act}
refer to the maximum and actual PoR’s proof size.

2.

Client-side Initiation
.
\mathtt{RCPoRP}.\mathtt{cInit}(1^{\lambda},u,{k^{\prime}},z,pl)

(a)

Calls
\mathtt{PoR.setup}(1^{\lambda},u)\rightarrow(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},pp)
to encode
u
. It appends
pp:=(\sigma,\phi,m,\zeta)
and
sk^{\prime}
to secret parameters
qp
.

(b)

Sets coin secret parameters
cp:=(o,o_{\scriptscriptstyle max},l,l_{\scriptscriptstyle max},z)
, then
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}=z\cdot(o_{\scriptscriptstyle max}+l_{\scriptscriptstyle max})
and
p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=z\cdot l_{\scriptscriptstyle max}
, given the price list
pl
, where
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
are the total number of masked coins
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{S}
should deposit. The parameters
pl,o,o_{\scriptscriptstyle max},l,l_{\scriptscriptstyle max},z
are explained in Section
III
.

(c)

Calls
\mathtt{SAP.init}(1^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},qp)\rightarrow(r_{\scriptscriptstyle qp},g_{\scriptscriptstyle qp},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SAP}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}})
and
\mathtt{SAP.init}(1^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda},

adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},cp)\rightarrow(r_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},g_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SAP}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}})
to initiate agreements on
qp
and
cp
with
\mathcal{S}
. Let
T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}:=(\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp},g_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})
and
T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}:=(\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},g_{\scriptscriptstyle cp})
, s.t.
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}:=(qp,r_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})
and
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}:=(cp,r_{\scriptscriptstyle cp})
are the openings of
g_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
and
g_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}
. Let
T:=\{T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp},T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}\}
.

(d)

Sets a smart contract, SC, that explicitly specifies parameters
z
,
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
,
p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
,
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SAP}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}}
,
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SAP}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}
,
pk^{\prime}
, including time values
\texttt{Time}:=\{\texttt{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0},...,\texttt{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,1},...,\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle z,2},\texttt{J},

\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 6},\texttt{L}\}
and a vector
[y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}]
initialized as
[0,0,0,0]
. It deploys SC. Let
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SC}}
be the address of the deployed SC and
\bm{y}:=[y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}]
.

(e)

Deposits
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
coins in the contract. It sends
u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},z,\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp},
and
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}
(along with
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SC}}
) to
\mathcal{S}
. Let
\texttt{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
be the time that the above process finishes.

3.

Server-side Initiation
.
\mathtt{RCPoRP}.\mathtt{sInit}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},z,T,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y})

(a)

Checks the parameters in
T
(e.g.,
qp
and
cp
) and in SC (e.g.,
p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},\bm{y}
) and ensures sufficient amount of coins has been deposited by
\mathcal{C}
.

(b)

Calls
\mathtt{SAP.agree}(qp,r_{\scriptscriptstyle qp},g_{\scriptscriptstyle qp},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SAP}_{1}})\rightarrow(g^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp},\\
b_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})
and
\mathtt{SAP.agree}(cp,r_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},g_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{SAP}_{2}})\hskip-2.84526pt\rightarrow(g^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},b_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
, to check and agree on
qp
and
cp
.

(c)

If
b_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=0
or
b_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=0
, it sets
a=0
. Otherwise, it verifies the public parameters correctness as follows (i) rebuilds the Merkle tree on
u^{\scriptscriptstyle*}
and checks the resulting root equals
\sigma
, and (ii) checks
|u^{*}|=m
and
\phi\leq m
, where
(m,\phi)\in T
, and
\sigma\in pp\in T
. If the checks pass, it sets
a=1
; else, it sets
a=0
. It sends
a
and
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
coins to SC at time
\texttt{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
, where
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=\bot
if
a=0
.

\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{C}
can withdraw their coins at time
\texttt{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, if
\mathcal{S}
sends
a=0
, fewer coins than
p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
, or nothing to the SC. To withdraw,
\mathcal{S}
or
\mathcal{C}
sends a “pay” message to
\mathtt{RCPoRP}.\mathtt{pay}(.)
at time
\texttt{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
.

Billing-cycles Onset
.
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{S}
engage in phases
4
-
6
, at the end of every
j
-th billing cycle, where
1\leq j\leq z
. Each
j
-th cycle includes two time points,
\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,1}
and
\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,2}
, where
\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,2}>\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,1}
, and
\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,1}>\texttt{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
.

4.

Client-side Query Generation
.

\mathtt{RCPoRP}.\mathtt{genQuery}(1^{\lambda},T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})

(a)

Calls
\mathtt{PoR.genQuery}(1^{\lambda},pp)\rightarrow\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, where
pp\in T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
.

(b)

Sends encryption
c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\mathtt{Enc}(\bar{k},\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})
to SC at time
\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,1}
.

5.

Server-side Proof Generation
.

\mathtt{RCPoRP}.\mathtt{prove}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})

(a)

Decrypts the query,
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\mathtt{Dec}(\bar{k},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})
, where
\bar{k}\in T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
.

(b)

Checks the query’s correctness by ensuring
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
is not empty, and is in the key’s universe, i.e.,
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\in\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle\psi}
. If the checks pass, it sets
b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=1
; else, it sets
b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=0
.

\bullet

if
b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=1
, it sets
m_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}=\bot
. It generates proofs vector by calling
\mathtt{PoR.prove}(u^{\scriptscriptstyle*},\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},pp)\rightarrow{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
. Then, it encrypts the proofs, i.e., for
1\leq g\leq|{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}|:\mathtt{Enc}(\bar{k},{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}[g])={\bm{\pi}}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}[g]
. Let
{\bm{\pi}}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
contain the encrypted proofs. It pads every encrypted proof in
{\bm{\pi}}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
with
{pad}_{\scriptscriptstyle\pi}\in T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
random values picked from the encryption’s output range,
U
. Let
\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
be the result. It sends
\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
to SC at time
\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,2}
.

\bullet

if
b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=0
, it sets the complaint
m_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}=j
. It constructs a dummy proof
{\bm{\pi}}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
with elements randomly picked from
U
, pads the result as above, and sends the result,
\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, to SC at time
\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,2}
.

It outputs
b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
and
m_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S},j}
.

6.

Client-side Proof Verification
.

\mathtt{RCPoRP}.\mathtt{verify}(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})

(a)

Removes the pads from
\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, yielding
\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
. It decrypts the service proofs
\mathtt{Dec}(\bar{k},\bm{\pi}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})=\bm{\pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
and then verifies the proof by calling
\mathtt{PoR.verify}(\bm{\pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},

\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},{pp})\rightarrow\bm{d}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, where
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\mathtt{Dec}(\bar{k},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})
.

\bullet

if
{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
passes the verification, i.e.,
{\bm{d}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}[0]=1
, it sets
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}=\bot
and concludes that the service for this verification was delivered.

\bullet

otherwise (i.e.,
{\bm{d}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}[0]=0
), it sets
g={\bm{d}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}[1]
and the complaint
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}=[j,g]
.
Recall,
{\bm{d}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}[1]
refers to a rejected proof’s index in proof vector
{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
.

(b)

It outputs
{\bm{d}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
and
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C},j}
.

7.

Dispute Resolution
.

\mathtt{RCPoRP}.\mathtt{resolve}(\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},z,{\bm{\pi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle*},{\bm{c}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}},T_{\scriptscriptstyle qp})

This phase takes place only in case of dispute, i.e., when
\mathcal{C}
rejects service proofs or
\mathcal{S}
rejects the queries.

(a)

The arbiter
\mathcal{R}
ensures counters:
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
and
y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
are set to
0
, before time
\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
, where
\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}>\texttt{G}_{\scriptscriptstyle z,2}+\texttt{J}
.

(b)

\mathcal{S}
sends complaints
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
and
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
to
\mathcal{R}
at time
\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
.

(c)

Upon receiving
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
and
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
,
\mathcal{R}
takes the following steps at time
\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
.

i.

checks
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
’s validity, by calling the SAP’s verification which returns
d
. If the output is
d=0
, it discards
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
and does not take steps
7(c)ii
and
7(c)iii
. Otherwise, it proceeds to the next step.

ii.

removes from
{\bm{m}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
any element duplicated or not in range
[1,z]
. It constructs an empty vector
{\bm{v}}
.

iii.

for any element
i\in{\bm{m}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
: fetches the related encrypted query
c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in\bm{c}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}
from SC and decrypts it as
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=\mathtt{Dec}(\bar{k},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
; it checks the query by doing the same checks performed in step
5b
. If the query is rejected, it increments
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
by
1
and appends
i
to
{\bm{v}}
. If the query is accepted, it increments
y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
by
1
. Let
\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
be the time the above checks are complete.

(d)

\mathcal{C}
sends complaints
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
to
\mathcal{R}
at time
\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
.

(e)

Upon receiving
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
,
\mathcal{R}
takes the below steps at time
\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
.

i.

checks
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
’s validity, by calling the SAP’s verification which returns
d^{\prime}
. If
d^{\prime}=0
, it discards
\bm{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
, and does not take steps
7(e)ii
-
7(e)iii
. Otherwise, it proceeds to the next step.

ii.

ensures each vector
{\bm{m}}\in{\bm{m}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
is valid. Specifically, it checks there exist no two vectors:
{\bm{m}},{\bm{m}}^{\prime}\in{\bm{m}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
such that
{\bm{m}}[0]={\bm{m}}^{\prime}[0]
. If such vectors exist, it deletes the redundant ones from
{\bm{m}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
. This ensures no two claims refer to the same verification. It removes any vector
{\bm{m}}
from
{\bm{m}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
if
{\bm{m}}[0]
is not in the range
[1,z]
or if
{\bm{m}}[0]\in{\bm{v}}
. This check ensures
\mathcal{C}
cannot hold
\mathcal{S}
accountable if
\mathcal{C}
generated an invalid query for the same verification.

iii.

for every vector
{\bm{m}}\in{\bm{m}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
:

A.

retrieves a rejected proof’s details by setting
j={\bm{m}}[0]
and
g={\bm{m}}[1]
. Recall that
g
refers to the index of a rejected proof in the proof vector generated for
j
-th verification, i.e.,
{\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
.

B.

fetches the related encrypted query
c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\in\bm{c}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}
from SC and decrypts it:
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\mathtt{Dec}(\bar{k},c^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})
. It removes the pads from
g
-th padded encrypted proof. Let
{\bm{\pi}}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}[g]
be the result. It decrypts the encrypted proof,
\mathtt{Dec}(\bar{k},{\bm{\pi}}^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}[g]))={\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}[g]
.

C.

identifies the misbehaving party as follows.

•

verifies
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
by doing the same checks done in step
5b
. If the checks do not pass, it sets
I_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\mathcal{C}
and skips the next two steps; otherwise, it proceeds to the next step.

•

derives the related challenged block index from
\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
:
q_{\scriptscriptstyle g}=\big(\mathtt{PRF}(\hat{k}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},g)\bmod m\big)+1
.

•

verifies only
g
-th proof, by calling
\mathtt{PoR.verify}({\bm{\pi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}[g],

q_{\scriptscriptstyle g},

pp)\rightarrow{\bm{d}}^{\prime}
. If
{\bm{d}}^{\prime}[0]=0
, it sets
I_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\mathcal{S}
. Else, it sets
I_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\bot
.

•

if
I_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\mathcal{C}
and
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
or
y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
was not incremented for
j
-th verification, it increments
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
by
1
. If
I_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\mathcal{S}
and
y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
was not incremented for
j
-th verification, it increments
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
by
1
. If
I_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\bot
and
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
was not incremented for
j
-th verification, it increments
y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
by
1
.

(f)

The arbiter at time
\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}
sends
[y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}]
to SC which accordingly adds them to
\bm{y}
.

8.

Coin Transfer
.
\mathtt{RCPoRP}.\mathtt{pay}(\bm{y},T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp},a,p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})

(a)

If SC receives “pay” message at time
\texttt{T}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, where
a=0
or
coins^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}<p_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
, then it sends
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
coins to
\mathcal{C}
and
coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
coins to
\mathcal{S}
. Otherwise (i.e., they reach an agreement), the following step is executed.

(b)

If SC receives “pay” message and
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}\in T_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}
at time
\texttt{L}>\texttt{K}_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}
, it checks
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle cp}
’s validity by calling the SAP’s verification which returns
d^{\prime\prime}
.

(c)

If
d^{\prime\prime}=1
, SC distributes the coins to the parties as follows:

i.

coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}=coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}-o\cdot(z-y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})-l\cdot(y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}+y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}})
coins to
\mathcal{C}
.

ii.

coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}=coin^{\scriptscriptstyle*}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}+o\cdot(z-y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})-l\cdot(y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}+y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}})
coins to
\mathcal{S}
.

iii.

coin_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{R}}=l\cdot(y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}+y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}+y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}+y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}})
coins to
\mathcal{R}
.

Briefly, the RC-PoR-P protocol’s correctness holds dues to the correctness of PoR, symmetric key encryption, SAP, and smart contract. Appendix
J
presents a more detailed discussion. Below, we state our main theorem on the security of the RC-PoR-P scheme. Appendix
K
presents the theorem’s proof.

Theorem 1
.

The RC-PoR-P scheme with functions
F_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}},M_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}},E_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}},

D_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}},Q_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{PoR}}
described in Subsections
IX-A
and
IX-B
is secure (cf. Definition
6
), if the underlying Merkle tree, pseudorandom function, commitment scheme, and digital signature scheme are secure, and the symmetric-key encryption scheme is IND-CPA secure.

IX-C

RC-PoR-P Without Arbiter’s Involvement

Due to the efficiency of the arbiter-side algorithm, i.e.,
\mathtt{RCSPoR.resolve}(\cdot)
, we can delegate the arbiter’s role to the smart contract, SC. In this case, the third-party arbiter’s involvement is no longer needed. But, to have the new variant of RC-PoR-P, we need to adjust the original RC-PoR-P’s protocol and definition, primarily from two perspectives. First, how a party pays to resolve a dispute would change, which ultimately affects the amount of coins each party receives in the coin transfer phase (see below for more details).
Second, there would be no need to keep track of the number of times a party unnecessarily raises a dispute, as it pays the contract when it sends a query, before the contract processes its claim. In Appendix
L-A
, we provide a generic definition for RC-S-P for the case where the arbiter’s role can be played by a smart contract. The generic definition also captures the new variant of RC-PoR-P.

Next, we elaborate on how the original RC-PoR-P protocol can be adjusted such that the third-party arbiter’s role is totally delegated to the smart contract, SC. Briefly, Phases
1
–
6
remain unchanged, with an exception. Namely, in step
2d
, only two counters
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
are created, instead of four counters; accordingly, in the same step, vector
\bm{y}
is now
\bm{y}:[y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}},y_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}]
, so counters
y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
are excluded from the vector. At a high level, the changes applied to phase
7
are as follows: the parties send their complaints to SC now, SC does not maintain
y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}
and
y^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}
anymore, SC takes the related steps (on the arbiter’s behalf), and it reads its internal state any time it needs to read data already stored on the contract. Moreover, the main adjustment to phase
8
is that the amount of coins each party receives changes. In the RC-PoR-P and RC-S-P (presented in sections
IX-B
and
G-A
, respectively), the party which raises a dispute does not pay the arbiter when it sends to it the dispute query. Instead, loosely speaking, the arbiter in the coin transfer phase is paid by a misbehaving party. In contrast, when the arbiter’s role is played by a smart contract, the party which raises a dispute and sends the dispute query to the contract (due to the nature of Ethereum smart contracts) has to pay the contract
before
the contract processes its query. This means that an honest party which sends a complaint to the contract needs to be compensated (by the corrupt party) for the amount of coins it sent to the contract to resolve the dispute.
In Appendix
L-B
, we present the modified RC-PoR-P protocol in more detail.

X
Performance Evaluation of RC-PoR-P

In this section, we provide an analysis of the RC-PoR-P protocol. Table
I
summarises the protocol’s concrete cost (we also provide a table for its asymptotic cost in Appendix
M
).
Also, we compare RC-PoR-P with (a) the zero-knowledge contingent (publicly verifiable) PoR payment in
[
15
]
and the fair PoR payment scheme in
[
1
]
that are more efficient than the state-of-the-art and closest to our work. Table
II
summarises the comparison. The analysis of RC-PoR-P covers both asymptotic and concrete overheads. To conduct the concrete cost study, we have implemented RC-PoR-P. The protocol’s off-chain and on-chain parts have been implemented in C++ and Solidity programming languages respectively. To conduct the off-chain experiment, we used a server with dual Intel Xeon Gold 5118, 2.30 GHz CPU and 256 GB RAM. To carry out the on-chain experiment, we used a MacBook Pro laptop with quad-core Intel Core i5, 2 GHz CPU and 16 GB RAM that interacts with the Ethereum private blockchain. We ran the experiment
10
times. In the experiment, we used the SHA-2 hash function and set its output length and the security parameter to
128
bits. We set the size of every block to
128
bits, as in
[
56
]
. We used a random file whose size is in the range
[64\ \text{MB},4\ \text{GB}]
. This results in the number of file blocks in the range
[2^{\scriptscriptstyle 22},2^{\scriptscriptstyle 28}]
. Since in the experiment we used relatively large file sizes, to lower on-chain transaction costs, we allow the parties to use the technique explained in Section
G-A
, which lets the server and client exchange the (PoR) proofs off-chain in an irrefutable fashion
4

\mathcal{S}

j

X-A

Computation Cost

In our analysis, the cost of erasure-coding a file is not taken into consideration, as it is identical in all PoR schemes. We first analyse the computation cost of RC-PoR-P.
\mathcal{C}
’s cost is as follows. In phase
2
, its cost in step
2a
involves
m\cdot\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(m)}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}\frac{1}{2^{\scriptscriptstyle i}}
invocations of a hash function. So its complexity in this step is
O(m)
. Its total cost in steps
2b
and
2c
involves two invocations of the hash function. Therefore, the client-side total complexity in this phase is
O(m)
. In this phase, its off-chain
run-time
increases about
2\times
(i.e., from
23.1
to
45.5,...,
from
732.1
to
1596.6
seconds) when
m
increases (i.e., from
2^{\scriptscriptstyle 22}
to
2^{\scriptscriptstyle 23},...,
from
2^{\scriptscriptstyle 27}
to
2^{\scriptscriptstyle 28}
blocks). This phase also costs it
123\cdot 10^{\scriptscriptstyle-5}
ether. In phase
4
,
\mathcal{C}
invokes
\mathtt{PRF}
and symmetric-key encryption
\phi
times and once respectively. So, for
z
verifications its total computation cost is
O(z\cdot\phi)
. Its off-chain run-time in this phase is negligibly small. This phase also costs it
6\cdot 10^{\scriptscriptstyle-5}\cdot z
ether. In phase
6
,
\mathcal{C}
for each verification decrypts and verifies proofs which mainly involves
\phi\cdot(\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(m)+1)
invocations of the symmetric key encryption and
\phi\cdot\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(m)
invocations of the hash function. So, its total complexity in this phase is
O(z\cdot\phi\cdot\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(m))
. Its off-chain run-time in this phase is very low and grows almost
1.1\times
(i.e., from
0.09\cdot z
to
0.11\cdot z,...,
from
0.21\cdot z
to
0.24\cdot z
seconds) when
m
increases.

Now, we analyse
\mathcal{S}
’s computation cost. In phase
3
,
\mathcal{S}
’s complexity is
O(m)
. Its off-chain run-time in this phase grows
2\times
(i.e., from
8.9
to
16.5,...,
from
248.8
to
548.8
seconds) when
m
increases. This phase costs it
9\cdot 10^{\scriptscriptstyle-5}
ether. In phase
5
,
\mathcal{S}
decrypts a value for each verification, generates and encrypts proofs that require
\phi\cdot\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(m)
invocations of the hash function and
\phi\cdot(\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(m)+1)
invocations of symmetric key encryption, for each verification. So, its total complexity in phase
5
is
O(z\cdot\phi\cdot\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(m))
. In this phase, its off-chain run-time grows about
2.1\times
(i.e., from
22.4\cdot z
to
30.4\cdot z,...,
from
793.1\cdot z
to
1820.7\cdot z
seconds) when
m
increases.
5
5

5

To determine
\mathcal{S}
’s cost for generating a proof, we considered the case where
\mathcal{S}
does not store the Merkle tree nodes (to save storage space), instead it generates the tree’s paths every time a challenge is given to it. If we let
\mathcal{S}
store the tree, then it would have a lower computation overhead.

Next, we analyse
\mathcal{R}
’s cost in phase
7
. First, we evaluate
\mathcal{R}
’s cost when it is invoked by an honest
\mathcal{S}
. In this case, it invokes the hash function twice and decrypts
|\bm{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}|
queries, where
|\bm{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}|
is the total number of verifications that
\mathcal{S}
complained about and
|\bm{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}|\leq z
. Now, we evaluate its cost when it is invoked by an honest
\mathcal{C}
. It invokes the hash function twice to check the correctness of the statement,
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle qp}
, sent by the client. It invokes the hash function
|\bm{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}|\cdot(\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(m)+2)
times and the symmetric key encryption
|\bm{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}|\cdot(\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(m)+2)
times, where
|\bm{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}|
is the total number of verifications that
\mathcal{C}
complained about. Thus, its cost, in phase
7
is at most
O(z^{\prime}\cdot\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(m))
, where
z^{\prime}=Max(|\bm{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{C}}|,|\bm{v}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}|)
and
z^{\prime}\leq z
. Note that due to the use of the proof of misbehaviour in the protocol,
\mathcal{R}
’s cost is about
\frac{1}{\phi}=\frac{1}{460}
of its computation cost in the absence of such technique where it has to check all
\phi
proofs for each verification.
6
6

6

As shown in
[
9
]
, to ensure 99% of file blocks is retrievable, it would be sufficient to set the number of challenged blocks to
460
.
Its off-chain run-time is very low and increases about
1.3\times
(i.e., from
2\cdot 10^{\scriptscriptstyle-5}\cdot z^{\prime}
to
4\cdot 10^{\scriptscriptstyle-5}\cdot z^{\prime},...,
from
9\cdot 10^{\scriptscriptstyle-5}\cdot z^{\prime}
to
